is drawn in the strict interior of
\alpha(v)
. If the cutter
c_{v}
intersects
u
or
u^{\prime}
, then both
u
and
u^{\prime}
are drawn inside
\alpha(v_{1})
or
\alpha(v_{2})
, a contradiction to
v
being lowermost. Therefore,
c_{v}
intersects neither
u
nor
u^{\prime}
. So,
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v_{1})
or
\alpha(v_{2})
; without loss of generality, assume that
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v_{1})
. In addition, due to
v
being a lowest vertex in
\cal{T}
such that
u
and
u^{\prime}
are drawn inside
\alpha(v)
,
u^{\prime}
is not drawn inside
\alpha(v_{1})
. Therefore,
u^{\prime}
is drawn inside
\alpha(v_{2})
, but not inside
\alpha(v_{1})
(see Figure
13
). Hence,
(c,f)
separates
\{u,u^{\prime}\}
, and so
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{VerAsso}(c_{v},\alpha(v))\subseteq\beta(v)
. Thus, we get that there exists
v\in V_{T}
such that
u,u^{\prime}\in\beta(v)
, and hence we are done in this case as well.

Now, we show that for every
u\in V
there exists
v\in V_{T}
such that
u\in\beta(v)
. This part of the proof is similar to the first part of the proof. Let
u\in V
. Let
v\in V_{T}
be a lowest vertex in
{\cal T}
such that
u
is drawn inside
\alpha(v)
, that is, for every descendant
\ell\in V_{T}
of
v
in
{\cal T}
, it follows that
u
is not drawn inside
\alpha(\ell)
. Observe that
u
is drawn inside
R_{d}
. So, the set of vertices
x\in V_{T}
in
{\cal T}
such that
u
is drawn inside
\alpha(x)
is not empty, and hence the choice of
v
is well defined. We first assume that
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v)
. In this case,
v
is not a leaf. Then, there exists a cutter
c_{v}
of
\alpha(v)=f
such that
\alpha(v_{1})=f_{1}(c)
and
\alpha(v_{2})=f_{2}(c)
, where
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are the children of
v
. Observe that if
c
intersects
u
, then
u
is drawn inside both
\alpha(v_{1})
and
\alpha(v_{2})
, a contradiction to
v
being lowermost. Otherwise,
c
does not intersect
u
, so
u
is drawn inside one of
\alpha(v_{1})
and
\alpha(v_{2})
; again, this is a contradiction to
v
being lowermost. Either way, we get a contradiction when we assume that
u
is drawn strictly inside
u
. Therefore,
u
is drawn on
\alpha(v)
, and then
u\in\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))\subseteq\beta(v)
. Therefore, we get that there exists
v\in V_{T}
such that
u,\in\beta(v)
, and we are done.
∎

Figure 13
:
The figure shows a case where
u
and
u^{\prime}
are drawn inside
f
, such that
u
is strictly inside
f_{1}(c)
and
u^{\prime}
is strictly outside
f_{1}(c)
. Therefore,
(c,f)
separates
\{u,u^{\prime}\}
.

(b)

(c)

Figure 14
:
Examples of some cases where
a
is an ancestor of both
v
and
v^{\prime}
in the frame-tree: (a)
u
is drawn on
(f^{v}\cup c_{v})\cap(f^{v^{\prime}}\cup c_{v^{\prime}})
; (b)
u
is drawn strictly inside
f^{a}_{2}(c_{a})
and
z
is drawn strictly inside
f^{v}
; (c)
u
is drawn strictly outside
f^{a}
and
z
is drawn strictly inside
f^{v}
.

Now, we will show that
\beta
satisfies another property of a tree decomposition. Specifically, we will show that for every
u\in V
, the induced subgraph
{\cal T}[V_{T}^{u}]
, where
V_{T}^{u}=\{v\in V_{T}~|~u\in\beta(v)\}
, is connected. Observe that Lemma
31
already asserts that
{\cal T}[V_{T}^{u}]
is non-empty.

Lemma 32
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a polyline grid drawing of
G
. Let
({\cal T}=(V_{T},E_{T}),\alpha:V_{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Frames})
be a frame-tree of
d
. Let
\beta:V_{T}\rightarrow 2^{V}
be the function defined as follows:
\beta(v)=\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))\cup\mathsf{VerAsso}(c_{v})
for every
v\in V_{T}
that is not a leaf, and
\beta(v)=\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))
for every
v\in V_{T}
that is a leaf. Then, for every
u\in V
, the induced subgraph
{\cal T}[V_{T}^{u}]
, where
V_{T}^{u}=\{v\in V_{T}~|~u\in\beta(v)\}
, is connected.

Proof.

Let
u\in V
. Assume, towards a contradiction, that
{\cal T}[V_{T}^{u}]
is not connected. Let
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{T}^{u}
be two vertices belonging to different connected components of
{\cal T}[V_{T}^{u}]
. Let
a\in V_{T}
be a vertex on the shortest path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
{\cal T}
such that
u\notin\beta(a)
. Then,
a
is an ancestor of at least one among
v
and
v^{\prime}
. Next, we consider two cases.

Case 1.
Assume first that
a
is an ancestor of both
v
and
v^{\prime}
(see Figure
5(a)
). Since
a
is a vertex on the shortest path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
in
{\cal T}
, then
a
is the lowest common ancestor of
v
and
v^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha(a)=f^{a}
. Now, by the definition of frame-tree,
a
has exactly two children,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, and there exists a cutter
c_{a}
of
f^{a}
such that
\alpha(a_{1})=f^{a}_{1}(c_{a})
and
\alpha(a_{2})=f^{a}_{2}(c_{a})
(see Figure
5(b)
). Since
a
is the lowest common ancestor of
v
and
v^{\prime}
, we get that one of
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
is an ancestor of
v
(or equal to
v
), and the other one is an ancestor of
v^{\prime}
(or equal to
v^{\prime}
). Without loss of generality, assume that
a_{1}
is an ancestor of
v
(or equal to
v
), and
a_{2}
is an ancestor of
v^{\prime}
(or equal to
v^{\prime}
) (see Figure
5(a)
). Observe that
\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(a_{1}))\cap\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(a_{2}))\subseteq\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(a))\cup\mathsf{GridPoints}(c_{a})
(see Figure
5(b)
), and, in turn, also
(\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(v))\cup\mathsf{GridPoints}(c_{v}))\cap(\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))\cup\mathsf{GridPoints}(c_{v^{\prime}}))\subseteq\mathsf{GridPoints}(\alpha(a))\cup\mathsf{GridPoints}(c_{a})
(see Figure
5(f)
). Therefore, if
u
is drawn on both
\alpha(v)\cup c_{v}
and
\alpha(v^{\prime})\cup c_{v^{\prime}}
, we get that
u
is drawn also
\alpha(a)\cup c_{a}
(see Figure
14(a)
). Thus, by definition,
u\in\beta(a)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
u\notin\beta(a)
. Therefore,
u
is not drawn on at least one among
\alpha(v)\cup c_{v}
and
\alpha(v^{\prime})\cup c_{v^{\prime}}
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
u
is not drawn
\alpha(v)\cup c_{v}
. Because
u\in\beta(v)
and since
u
is not drawn on
\alpha(v)
, there must be an edge
\{u,z\}\in E
such that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(v)
or by
(c_{v},\alpha(v))
in
d
. We have the following cases.

•

If
u
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a_{1})
, then
\{u,z\}
is not separated by
(c_{v},\alpha(v))
; so, it is separated by
\alpha(v)
. Therefore,
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v)
. We have the following two sub-cases.

–

If
u
is drawn inside
\alpha(a_{2})
, then
(c_{a},\alpha(a))
separates
\{u,z\}
. So, by definition,
u\in\beta(a)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
u\notin\beta(a)
(see Figure
14(b)
).

–

Otherwise,
u
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a_{2})
. Then, it is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a)
. So,
\alpha(a)
separates
\{u,z\}
, and then, by definition,
u\in\beta(a)
. This is, again, a contradiction to the assumption that
u\notin\beta(a)
(see Figure
14(c)
).

•

Otherwise,
u
is drawn inside
\alpha(a_{1})
. Then,
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(a_{1})
, since
\alpha(a_{1})\subseteq\alpha(a)\cup c_{a}
, and we assumed that
u\notin\beta(a)
. Thus,
u
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a_{2})
. So, this case is symmetric to the previous case, where
u
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a_{1})
. Therefore, we get a contradiction in this case as well.

Figure 15
:
Example of a case where
a
is an ancestor of
v
,
u
is drawn strictly outside
f^{a}
and
z
is drawn strictly inside
f^{v}
. The blue and the purple cutters correspond to vertices on the path from
a
to
v
in the corresponding frame-tree.

Case 2.
Now, assume that
a
is an ancestor of
v
but not an ancestor of
v^{\prime}
(the other case, where
a
is an ancestor of
v^{\prime}
but not an ancestor of
v
, is symmetric). We have the following cases.

Case 2(i).
Assume that
u
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a)
. Since
\alpha(v)
and
c_{v}
are bounded by
\alpha(a)
, then
u
is drawn on neither
\alpha(v)
nor
c_{v}
. Since
u\in\beta(v)
, there must be an edge
\{u,z\}\in E
such that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(v)
or by
(c_{v},\alpha(v))
in
d
. Now,
u
is not drawn inside
\alpha(v)
. So,
\{u,z\}
is not separated by
(c_{v},\alpha(v))
, which means that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(v)
. Therefore,
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v)
; so,
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(a)
. We get that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(a)
(see Figure
15
), and then, by the definition of
\beta
,
u\in\beta(a)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
a\notin V_{T}^{u}
.

Case 2(ii).
Assume that
u
is drawn inside
\alpha(a)
. If
u
is drawn on
\alpha(a)
, then, by the definition of
\beta
,
u\in\beta(a)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
a\notin V_{T}^{u}
. So,
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(a)
. Now, observe that since
a
is not an ancestor of
v^{\prime}
, then either
v^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
a
, or
v^{\prime}
and
a
are incomparable.

•

If
v^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
a
, then, since
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(a)
,
u
is drawn on neither
\alpha(v^{\prime})
nor
c_{v^{\prime}}
.

•

If
v^{\prime}
and
a
are incomparable, then, again, since
u
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(a)
,
u
is drawn on neither
\alpha(v^{\prime})
nor
c_{v^{\prime}}
.

Therefore, either way, we get that
u
is drawn on neither
\alpha(v^{\prime})
nor
c_{v^{\prime}}
.

Now, since
u\in\beta(v^{\prime})
, there must be an edge
\{u,z\}\in E
such that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(v^{\prime})
or by
(c_{v^{\prime}},\alpha(v^{\prime}))
in
d
. We have the following cases.

•

If
z
is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(a)
, then we get that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
\alpha(a)
; so, by definition,
u\in\beta(a)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
a\notin V_{T}^{u}
(see Figure
16
).

•

Otherwise,
z
is drawn inside
\alpha(a)
. We have the following two sub-cases.

(a)

(b)

Figure 16
:
Example of a case where
a
is an ancestor of
v
,
v^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
a
,
u
is drawn inside
f^{a}
and
z
is drawn strictly outside
f^{a}
. The blue and purple cutters correspond to vertices on the path from
a
to
v
in the corresponding frame-tree.

–

If
v^{\prime}
and
a
are incomparable, then, since
u
and
z
are drawn inside
\alpha(a)
, we get that
\{u,z\}
is separated by neither
\alpha(v^{\prime})
nor
(c_{v^{\prime}},\alpha(v^{\prime}))
. This is a contradiction to the assumption that
\{u,z\}
is separated by either
\alpha(v^{\prime})
or
(c_{v^{\prime}},\alpha(v^{\prime}))
.

–

Otherwise,
v^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
a
. Let
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
v^{\prime}_{2}
be the two children of
v^{\prime}
. Since
u
and
z
are drawn inside
\alpha(v)
, and since
\alpha(v)
is bounded by
\alpha(v^{\prime})
,
u
and
z
are drawn inside
\alpha(v^{\prime})
. So,
\{u,z\}
is not separated by
\alpha(v^{\prime})
, which means that
\{u,z\}
is separated by
(c_{v^{\prime}},\alpha(v^{\prime}))
. Therefore, by the definition of a cutter being separator of an edge, there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that one of
u
or
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{i})
(and hence it is not drawn on
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2\},j\neq i
), and the other vertex is not drawn inside
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{i})
. So, one among
u
and
z
is not drawn inside
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{1})
, and one among
u
and
z
is not drawn inside
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{2})
. Now, since
v^{\prime}
is an ancestor of
a
,
\alpha(v)
is bounded by either (and possibly equal to)
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{1})
or
\alpha(v^{\prime}_{2})
. So, one among
u
and
z
is not drawn inside
\alpha(v)
, a contradiction to the assumption that
u
and
z
are both drawn inside
\alpha(v)
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Now, from Lemmas
31
and
32
we can conclude that
({\cal T},\beta)
is a tree decomposition of
G
, as we state in the following corollary.

Corollary 33
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a polyline grid drawing of
G
. Let
({\cal T}=(V_{T},E_{T}),\alpha:V_{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Frames})
be a frame-tree of
d
. Let
\beta:V_{T}\rightarrow 2^{V}
be the function defined as follows:
\beta(v)=\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))\cup\mathsf{VerAsso}(c_{v},\alpha(v))
for every
v\in V_{T}
that is not a leaf, and
\beta(v)=\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))
for every
v\in V_{T}
that is a leaf. Then,
(\cal{T},\beta)
is a tree decomposition of
G
.

5
Relation to Treewidth

Equipped with Corollary
33
, we would like to prove a lower bound on the drawn treewidth of a drawing based on the treewidth of the graph. Let
G
be a graph, let
d
be a polyline grid drawing of
G
, and let
({\cal T}=(V_{T},E_{T}),\alpha:V_{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Frames})
be a frame-tree of
d
. In Lemma
34
ahead, we show that for every
v\in V_{T}
that is not a leaf, it follows that
|\beta(v)|\leq 2\cdot(\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2})))
, where
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are the two children of
v
in
\cal{T}
. To this end, we prove that every
u\in\beta(v)
contributes at least
\frac{1}{2}
to the sum
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2}))
. In addition, for every
v\in V_{T}
that is a leaf, we show that
|\beta(v)|\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))
. We are now ready to prove Lemma
34
.

Lemma 34
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a polyline grid drawing of
G
. Let
({\cal T}=(V_{T},E_{T}),\alpha:V_{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Frames})
be a frame-tree of
d
. Let
(\cal{T},\beta)
be the tree decomposition of
G
defined in Corollary
33
. Let
v\in V_{T}
. If
v
is an internal vertex, then
|\beta(v)|\leq 2\cdot(\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2})))
, where
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are the two children of
v
in
\cal{T}
. Otherwise,
v
is a leaf, and then
|\beta(v)|\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))
.

Proof.

Assume that
v
is not a leaf, and let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be the two children of
v
in
\cal{T}
(the proof where
v
is a leaf is similar). By the definition of the function
\beta
,
\beta(v)=\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))\cup\mathsf{VerAsso}(c_{v},\alpha(v))
. First, let
u\in\mathsf{VerAsso}(\alpha(v))
. Then, by the definition of the vertices associated with a frame (see Definition
22
), we have two cases.

•

u
is drawn on
\alpha(v)
in
d
. In this case, by the definition of the contribution of a vertex to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))
(see Definition
25
),
u
contributes at least
1
to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))
.

•

There exists an edge
\{u,z\}
that is separated by
\alpha(v)
in
d
. Therefore, one among
u
and
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v)
and the other is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(v)
, in
d
. Observe that, in this case, there must be at least one point
p\in d(\{u,z\})
such that
(p,\{u,z\})
is a turning point in
\alpha(v)
. So, in this case,
u
contributes at least
\frac{1}{2}
to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))
.

Second, let
u\in\mathsf{VerAsso}(c_{v},\alpha(v))
. Then, by the definition of the vertices associated with a cutter (see Definition
23
), we have two cases.

•

u
is drawn on
c_{v}
in
d
. So,
u
is drawn on both
\alpha(v_{1})
and
\alpha(v_{2})
, in
d
. Therefore, in this case, by the definition contribution,
u
contributes at least
1
to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))
and at least
1
to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2}))
.

•

There exists an edge
\{u,z\}
that is separated by
(c_{v},\alpha(v))
in
d
. Therefore, by the definition of the separation of an edge by a cutter, there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that one among
u
and
z
is drawn strictly inside
\alpha(v_{i})
in
d
, and the other one is drawn strictly outside
\alpha(v_{i})
in
d
. Hence, by the definition of the separation of an edge by a frame,
\alpha(v_{i})
separates
\{u,z\}
in
d
. In this case, notice that there must be at least one turning point of the edge
\{u,z\}
in
\alpha(v_{i})
. So, by the definition of contribution, in this case,
u
contributes at least
\frac{1}{2}
to
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{i}))
.

In conclusion, we have proved that every
u\in\beta(v)
that is an internal vertex contributes at least
\frac{1}{2}
to the sum
\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2}))
. So,
|\beta(v)|\leq 2\cdot(\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{1}))+\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v_{2})))
.
∎

Recall that the width of a frame-tree
({\cal T},\alpha)
is
\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)=\max\{\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))~|~v\in V_{T}\}
. In addition, the width of a tree decomposition
(\cal{T},\beta)
is
\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\beta)=\max_{v\in V_{T}}|\beta(v)|-1
. From Lemma
34
, we conclude that, for every
v\in V_{T}
,
|\beta(v)|\leq 6\cdot\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)
. So,
\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\beta)\leq 6\cdot\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)
(for any frame-tree
({\cal T},\alpha)
and its corresponding tree decomposition
({\cal T},\beta)
). Thus,
\frac{1}{6}\cdot\mathsf{tw}(G)\leq\mathsf{dtw}(d)
. In particular, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 35
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a polyline grid drawing of
G
. Then,
\mathsf{tw}(G)\leq\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{dtw}(d))
.

6
Problem Solving Scheme Using Drawn Tree Decompositions

In this section, we present our scheme for solving problems, based on our notion of a drawn tree decomposition. This scheme will be useful for various graph drawing and recognition problems.

Briefly, let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
f_{\mathsf{init}}
be a frame. Assume that we would like to construct a polyline grid drawing
d
of
G
such that
d
is strictly bounded by
f_{\mathsf{init}}
and has some specific properties, or return “no” if no such a polyline grid drawing exists.
Our scheme works as follows. Observe that if such a drawing
d
exists, it has a frame-tree of width
\mathsf{dtw}(d)
. We iterate over the potential values
k
for
\mathsf{dtw}(d)
, and aim to “guess” a drawing
d^{\prime}
of
G
and a frame-tree
({\cal T},\alpha)
of
d^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{width}(d^{\prime},({\cal T},\alpha))\leq k
.

For a given
k
, we try to “build” the drawing
d^{\prime}
, using dynamic programming. First, we begin with “leaf frames”, that is, frames with no grid points in their interior. Then, we continue with frames in order by increasing “size”, where “size” refers to the number of grid points in their interior. We “solve” each one of these frames. That is, for every frame
f
, we guess some specific information: we guess exactly how the intersection of the frame
f
with the drawing
d^{\prime}
“looks like” and what vertices and edges or “parts” of edges are drawn inside
f
. Our guess should provide that the width of
f
in any frame-tree of any drawing with the same specific information be bounded by
k
. We would like to know, for each guess, if there exists a “partial” drawing with the specific information we had guessed. Assume that we guessed a frame
f
that is not a leaf in any drawn frame decomposition of
d^{\prime}
, that is, a frame that has a grid point in its interior. Then, we can guess the cutter of
f
,
c
, in the frame-tree, and the specific information regarding the two frames associated with
c
,
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
. Then, because in the interior of each of
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
there are less points than in their interior of
f
, we have already “solved”
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
with the “specific information” guess. So, by looking up an already computed entry of the dynamic programming table, we try to “glue” the “partial drawings” for the specific guess of
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
, in order to construct a partial drawing for our guess for
f
, or conclude that there is no such partial drawing.

6.1
G^{*}
-Drawings

In order to present and prove the correctness of our scheme, we introduce a few definitions. As we explained in the beginning of Section
6
, we aim to “solve” small parts of potential drawings. Assume that
d
is a polyline grid drawing of a graph
G=(V,E)
, and let
f
be a frame. Observe that the part of
d
that is bounded by
f
, that is, the
sub-drawing
of
d
that is bounded by
f
, might contain “parts” of edges. In order to define such sub-drawings as drawings, we define the term
G^{*}
-drawing
. Intuitively, a
G^{*}
-drawing is a drawing that might contain “parts” of edges of
G
, represented by new “dummy” vertices, where the set of dummy vertices is denoted by
V^{*}
. In particular, for every edge
\{u,v\}
, vertices of the form
uv_{i}\in V^{*}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, will represent the turning points (see Definition
19
) of the edge
\{u,v\}
in
f
in the sub-drawing. By “making” the turning points vertices, we will be able to define this kind of sub-drawings as drawings.
Now, assume that the choices for
i\in\mathbb{N}
represent the order of appearance of the turning points of the edge
\{u,v\}
, starting from
u
. Then,
uv_{i}
and
uv_{i+1}
are connected by a “part” of the edge
\{u,v\}
in
d
; so, we will connect them with a dummy edge (e.g., see the edge
\{uv_{2},uv_{3}\}
in Figure
18
). We will denote the set of these dummy edges by
E^{*}
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. We assume that the set of vertices
V
is an ordered set, that is, for every
u,v\in V
,
v\neq u
, either
u<v
or
v<u
. First, for an edge
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u<v
, we define the set of dummy vertices as
V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\{uv_{i}~|~i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. In addition, we define the set of dummy edges of
\{u,v\}
as
E^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\{\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}~|~i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Now, we are ready to define the sets
V^{*}
and
E^{*}
.

Definition 36

(
V^{*}
and
E^{*}
).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. Then:

•

V^{*}=\bigcup_{\{u,v\}\in E,u<v}V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}
.

•

E^{*}=\bigcup_{\{u,v\}\in E,u<v}E^{*}_{\{u,v\}}
.

Recall that our intention is to define “parts” of drawings as drawings. For this purpose, we need to define the sub-graph that is drawn in a sub-drawing. We denote such a sub-graph by a pair
(U,F)
, where
U
is the set of vertices and
F
is the set of edges. In what follows, we aim to define which
(U,F)
is a
valid
pair, that is, which
(U,F)
can represent a sub-graph that we may encounter. As the sub-drawings that we encounter contain vertices and edges from the sets
V\cup V^{*}
and
E\cup E^{*}
, respectively, we demand that
U\subset V\cup V^{*}
, and
F\subset E\cup E^{*}
, where the endpoints of the edges in
F
belong to
U
(see Condition
1
in Definition
37
). In Condition
2
, for every
u,v\in U
such that
\{u,v\}\in E
, we consider two different cases. The first one is when the edge
\{u,v\}
is not intersected by the frame
f
, except maybe at its endpoints (e.g., see the edge
\{x,w\}
in Figure
18
), or
\{u,v\}
is drawn on
f
. In this case,
\{u,v\}
has no turning points other than, possibly,
(d(u),\{u,v\})
or
(d(v),\{u,v\})
; so, we do not need to use any of the dummy vertices of
\{u,v\}
, and hence
U\cap V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\emptyset
(Condition
2a
). In the other case,
\{u,v\}
intersects
f
in a point (or points) that is not its endpoints and
\{u,v\}
is not drawn on
f
. In this case,
\{u,v\}
has turning points other than
(d(u),\{u,v\})
or
(d(v),\{u,v\})
; so, we have a dummy vertex of
\{u,v\}
for every such turning point (e.g., see the edges
\{u,v\}
and
\{s,t\}
in Figure
18
). Specifically, we have that
U\cap V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\{uv_{1},uv_{2},\ldots,uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}\}
(Condition
2(b)i
). Now, we demand that the numbering of the dummy vertices corresponds the order of appearance of the turning points that they represent, from
u
to
v
. Then,
u
should be connected by an edge to
uv_{1}
,
v
should be connected by an edge to
uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}
, and for every
1\leq j\leq\mathsf{index}(u,v)-1
,
uv_{j}
should be connected by an edge to
uv_{j+1}
(Condition
2(b)ii
). Here, we have
\subseteq
rather than
=
since some of the aforementioned edges can be outside
f
(e.g., see the edges
\{s,st_{1}\}
and
\{st_{1},st_{2}\}
in Figure
18
). In addition, in this case, the edge
\{u,v\}
does not appear in the drawing (but only parts of this edge appear), and so
\{u,v\}\notin F
(Condition
2(b)iii
); e.g., see the edge
\{s,t\}
in Figure
18
.

Definition 37

(
Valid
(U,F)
).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A pair
(U,F)
is valid if the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

U\subset V\cup V^{*}
, and
F\subset E\cup E^{*}
where the endpoints of the edges in
F
belong to
U
.

2.

For every
u,v\in U
such that
\{u,v\}\in E
, exactly one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(a)

U\cap U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\emptyset
.

(b)

There exists
\mathsf{index}(u,v)\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

i.

U\cap V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}=\{uv_{1},uv_{2},\ldots,uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}\}
.

ii.

F\cap E^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\subseteq\{\{u,uv_{1}\}\}\cup\{\{uv_{j},uv_{j+1}\}~|~1\leq j\leq\mathsf{index}(u,v)-1\}\cup\{\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},

v\}\}
.

iii.

\{u,v\}\notin F
.

Let
d
be a drawing. We denote by
V(d)
and
E(d)
the vertex set and the edge set associated with
d
, respectively. Sometimes we denote
u
by
uv_{0}
and
v
by
uv_{{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}+1}
. Let
f
be a frame. We denote the set of all
P\in{\cal P}
such that
P
is strictly inside
f
by
{\cal P}(f)
(e.g., see the blue path in Figure
17
). We define an
almost straight-line path in
f
as a plane path, where i) the endpoints of the path are mapped to points in
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f)
, ii) the internal vertices are mapped to grid points strictly inside
f
, and iii) every edge is mapped to the line segment
s
connecting the images of their endpoints, and there exist
a,b\in\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
such that
s
is on
\ell(a,b)
(e.g., see the red path in Figure
17
). We denote by
{\cal P}^{*}(f)
the set of all almost straight line paths in
f
.

Figure 17
:
Examples of a path in
{\cal P}(f)
, drawn in blue, and a path in
{\cal P}^{*}(f)
, drawn in red.

Now, we are ready to define a
G^{*}
-drawing
. First, a
G^{*}
-drawing is a drawing of a graph that satisfies the conditions of Definition
37
, that is,
(V(d),E(d))
is valid (Condition
1
in Definition
38
). Second, we present the conditions needed in order to verify that sub-drawings are sub-drawings of a potential polyline grid drawing (see Definition
13
). Every vertex from the set
V
should be drawn on a grid point, as in a polyline grid drawing. On the other hand, a vertex
uv_{i}
from the set
V^{*}
represents a turning point of the edge
\{u,v\}
in a frame
f
, so
uv_{i}
is drawn on points from the set
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
(Condition
2
). Similarly, every edge
\{u,v\}\in E
should be drawn as a path from the set
{\cal P}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, as in a polyline grid drawing. Now, consider an edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)\cap E^{*}
, that is,
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
represents a part of the edge
\{u,v\}
. Observe that, from Condition
2
, vertices from
V^{*}
can be drawn on a point in
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. Thus, the endpoints of the drawn of the edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
are drawn on points from the set
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. In addition, every part of a drawing of an edge, that is, a part of a
p\in{\cal P}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, satisfies that every edge of
p
is mapped to the line segment connecting the images of their endpoints, which are from the set
\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. Thus,
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})\in{\cal P}^{*}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
(Condition
3
). Now, recall that in the definition of a polyline grid drawing (Definition
13
), we demand that an edge in the drawing does not intersect itself. Therefore, in Conditions
4
,
5
and
6
we demand that the “parts” of the edge
\{u,v\}
are non-intersecting (e.g., see the edges
\{st_{2},st_{3}\}
and
\{st_{3},t\}
in Figure
18
).

Definition 38

(
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A drawing
d
is a
G^{*}
-drawing if the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

(V(d),E(d))
is valid.

2.

For every
u\in V(d)\cap V
,
d(u)\in\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, and for every
u\in V(d)\cap V^{*}
,
d(u)\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
.

3.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E(d)\cap E
,
d(\{u,v\})\in{\cal P}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, and for every
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)\cap E^{*}
,
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})\in{\cal P}^{*}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
.

4.

For every
uv_{i},uv_{j}\in V^{*}
such that
i\neq j
,
d(uv_{i})\neq d(uv_{j})
.

5.

For every
uv_{j}\in V(d)
and
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)
,
j\neq i,i+1
,
d
does not draw
uv_{j}
on
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})
.

6.

Every two edges
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\},\{uv_{j},uv_{j+1}\}\in E(d)
,
i\neq j
, do not intersect internally.

In the rest of this subsection, we aim to extend some of the definitions in Section
3
, to be compatible with the definition of a
G^{*}
-drawing.

Figure 18
:
An example of a
G^{*}
-drawing. A frame
f
is drawn in orange and a cutter
c
of
f
is drawn in green.

First, we extend Definition
19
, the definition of turning points. Recall that we use
V^{*}
to define sub-drawings as drawings, but the vertices in
V^{*}
do not appear in the original drawing. Therefore, we would not like to refer to a point
d(uv_{i})
, where
uv_{i}\in V^{*}
, as a turning point in the partial drawing in a frame
f
, unless it is a turning point also in the original drawing. For example, see the point
d(st_{3})
in Figure
18
. Observe that
(d(st_{3}),\{s,t\})
is a turning point of
f
, but
(d(st_{3}),\{s,t\})
is not a turning point of the left subframe created by the cutter
c
. Technically, we refer to a dummy edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E^{*}
not as to a standalone edge, but as part of the edge
\{u,v\}
, and we refer to a dummy vertex
uv_{i}\in V^{*}
as a “point” on the edge
\{u,v\}
. Recall that a turning point of an edge
\{u,v\}
in
f
is an intersection point of
f
and
d(\{u,v\})
, such that “right after” or “right before”
p
there is no intersection between
d(\{u,v\})
and
f
. We split the definition into three parts corresponding to three cases regarding the point
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)
. If
p\notin\{d(uv_{i}),d(uv_{i+1})\}
, the terms right after or right before
p
are well defined on
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})
. In this case, if
(p,\{u,v\})
is indeed a turning point, we call it a
middle turning point
(see Definition
41
), e.g. see the red point
p
in Figure
19
. Now, assume that
p\in\{d(uv_{i}),d(uv_{i+1})\}
, and, without loss of generality, that
p=d(uv_{i})
. Then, the term right after
p
is well defined on
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})
, but the term right before is not, since
d(uv_{i})
is the “left” endpoint of
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})
. So, we can conclude that the drawings of
\{u,v\}
and
f
do not intersect right before
p
if one of two conditions satisfied:

•

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\notin E(d)
.

•

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\in E(d)
and right before
d(uv_{i})
,
d(\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\})
and
f
do not intersect.

In this case, if
(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point, we call it a
left turning point
(see Definition
39
); e.g., see the red points
d(uv_{2})
and
d(st_{2})
in Figure
19
. Observe that if
i=0
, that is,
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}=\{u,uv_{1}\}
, then trivially
\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}=\{uv_{-1},v\}\notin E(d)
, so
(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point as expected. The other case, where
p=d(uv_{i+1})
, is symmetric, and we call this kind of turning points
right turning points
(see Definition
40
); e.g., see the red points
d(st_{2})
and
d(st_{3})
in Figure
19
. At last,
(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point in
f
if
(p,\{u,v\})
is a left, right or middle turning point (see Definition
42
).

In the following definitions, we use the following notation: let
a
and
b
be two points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
\epsilon>0
; then, recall (from Section
3
) that we denote
\ell(a,a_{\epsilon})
by
\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(a,b)
, where
a_{\epsilon}
is the point on the line
\ell(a,b)
at distance
\epsilon
from
a
if it exists.

Definition 39

(
Left Turning Point in a
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing. Let
f
be a frame and let
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)
. Then,
(d(uv_{i}),\{u,v\})
is a left turning point in
f
if there exists
\epsilon>0
such that at least one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p^{\prime}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
, where
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})=(p,p^{\prime},\ldots,d(uv_{i+1}))
.

•

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\notin E(d)
.

•

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\in E(d)
and
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p^{\prime\prime}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
, where
d(\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\})=(d(uv_{i+1}),\ldots,p^{\prime\prime},p)
.

Figure 19
:
An illustration of left, right and middle turning points (drawn in red) in a
G^{*}
drawing in a frame. Specifically,
(d(uv_{2}),\{u,v\})
is a left turning point in
f
;
(d(st_{2}),\{s,t\})
is both a left and a right turning point in
f
;
(d(st_{3}),\{s,t\})
is a right turning point in
f
;
p
is a middle turning point in
f
.

Definition 40

(
Right Turning Point in a
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing. Let
f
be a frame and let
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)
. Then,
(d(uv_{i+1}),\{u,v\})
is a right turning point in
f
if there exists
\epsilon>0
such that at least one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p^{\prime}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
, where
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})=(d(uv_{i})\ldots,p^{\prime},p)
.

•

\{uv_{i+1},uv_{i+2}\}\notin E(d)
.

•

\{uv_{i+1},uv_{i+2}\}\in E(d)
and
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p^{\prime\prime}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
, where
d(\{uv_{i+1},uv_{i+2}\})=(p,p^{\prime\prime},\ldots d(uv_{i+2}))
.

Definition 41

(
Middle Turning Point in a
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing. Let
f
be a frame and let
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)
. Let
p\in(\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}

(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))\setminus\{d(uv_{i}),d(uv_{i+1})\}
be a point. Let
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
be the two vertices of the path
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i}+1\})=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})
such that
j>i
,
p\notin\{p_{i},p_{j}\}
and
(p_{i},\ldots,p_{j})
is the minimum size subpath containing
p
2
2

2

Observe that
j\in\{i+1,i+2\}
.
. Then,
(p,\{u,v\})
is a middle turning point in
f
if there exists
\epsilon>0
such that at least one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p_{i}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
.

•

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(\mathsf{line}_{\epsilon}(p,p_{j}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)=\{p\}
.

Definition 42

(
Turning Point in a
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G
be a graph and let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing. Let
f
be a frame and let
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d)
. Let
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d(\{uv_{i},

uv_{i+1}\}))\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)
be a point. Then,
(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

•

(p,\{u,v\})
is a left turning point.

•

(p,\{u,v\})
is a right turning point.

•

(p,\{u,v\})
is a middle turning point.

The definition for the number of turning points in
f
,
\mathsf{TurPoints}(f)
, remains unchanged, that is,
\mathsf{TurPoints}(f)=|\{(p,\{u,v\})~|~p\in P(f),\{u,v\}\in E,(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point in
f\}|
.

Moreover, the definition for the cost of a frame (Definition
24
) remains unchanged for
G^{*}
-drawings, as stated next:

Definition 43

(
Cost of a Frame in a
G^{*}
-Drawing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing and let
f
be a frame. The cost of
f
in
d
, denote by
\mathsf{cost}(f)
, is the sum of the number of vertices of
f^{\mathsf{min}}
, the number of vertices of
G
on
f
with respect to
d
and
\mathsf{TurnPoints}(f)
.

Lastly, we aim to extend Definition
29
. Observe that in Condition
1
of this definition, we demand that the frame associated with
v_{r}
is
R_{d}
. As we will see later, for every other frame
f
that strictly bounds
d
, we will get the same value for
\mathsf{dtw(d)}
if we change Definition
29
in a way that the frame that is associated with
v_{r}
, the “initial frame”, is
f
. Nevertheless, we might get a different value for
\mathsf{dtw(d)}
if we take two frames
f
and
f^{\prime}
that bound (but not necessarily strictly bound)
d
as the initial frames. Moreover, recall that in our scheme, we aim to build (partial) drawing, with a bounded
\mathsf{dtw}
, by gluing two smaller parts of that (partial) drawing. To control
\mathsf{dtw(d)}
, we would like to build a frame-tree of the glued drawing, based on the frame-trees we should already have for the smaller parts. So, we extend Definition
29
to be compatible with both a
G^{*}
-drawing and an initial frame other than
R_{d}
.

Definition 44

(
f
-Frame-Tree).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing and let
f
be a boundary frame of
d
. An
f
-frame-tree of
d
is a pair
({\cal T}=(V_{T},E_{T}),\alpha:V_{T}\rightarrow\mathsf{Frames})
where
\cal T
is a binary rooted tree, and:

1.

\alpha(v_{r})=f
, where
v_{r}
is the root of
\cal T
.

2.

For every internal vertex
v\in V_{T}
, there exists a cutter
c_{v}
of
f
such that
\alpha(v_{1})=f_{1}(c_{v})
and
\alpha(v_{2})=f_{2}(c_{v})
, where
\alpha(v)=f
and
v_{1},v_{2}
are the children of
v
in
{\cal T}
. We say that
c_{v}
is the cutter associated with
v
.

3.

A vertex
v\in V_{T}
is a leaf if and only if there are no grid points in the interior of
\alpha(v)
.

The
width
of an
f
-drawn tree frame, denoted by
\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)
, is the maximum cost of a frame in
d
, i.e.,
\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)=\max\{\mathsf{cost}(\alpha(v))~|~v\in V_{T}\}
.
The
f
-drawn treewidth
of
d
, denoted by
\mathsf{dtw}(d,f)
, is minimum width of an
f
-drawn tree frame of
d
, i.e.,
\mathsf{dtw}(d,f)=\min\{\mathsf{width}({\cal T},\alpha)~|~({\cal T},\alpha)
is an
f
-drawn tree frame of
d\}
.

6.2
Info-Frames

In this subsection we introduce the term
info-frame
. An info-frame is a way for us to describe a minimal amount of information we need to store for small parts of a drawing in order to glue two parts together. We begin with the first property of an info-frame. Assume that we have a part of a drawing
d
, that is, a
G^{*}
-drawing, bounded by a frame
f
. First, we would like to know exactly how the boundary of the drawing looks like, that is, the drawing that is “on”
f
, denoted by
d_{f}
. Second, we would like to know the set of vertices that are drawn strictly inside
f
, denoted by
U_{f}
. Also, we would like to know the set of edges with at least one endpoint in
V(d_{f})
that we have in the partial drawing, denoted by
E_{f}
. Now, recall that in polyline grid drawings edges are drawn as paths from the set
\cal{P}
. So, for every
\{u,v\}\in E
,
d(\{u,v\})
is a drawn straight line path, whose vertices are on grid points. In particular, bends in
d(\{u,v\})
can be only on grid points. In light of this, consider a vertex
uv_{i}
drawn on a point from the set
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. We need to store the “direction” of
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E^{*}
(or
\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\in E^{*}
), which represents a part of the edge
\{u,v\}
, in order to verify that the other part of the edge has the same direction, so there is no bending at the point
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})
.

In Definition
45
ahead, we state the conditions that a tuple
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
should satisfy in order to have the first property an info-frame. The intuition behind these conditions is as follows.

Conditions
1
,
2
and
3
.
First, we state, as explained, that
f
is a frame,
d_{f}
is a
G^{*}
-drawing on
f
,
U_{f}\subseteq V
, and
E_{f}\subseteq\{\{u,v\}\in E^{*}\cup E~|~u\in V(d_{f}),v\in V(d_{f})\cup U_{f}\}
. In addition, we expect that the vertices in
V^{*}
represent the turning points in
f
(e.g., see vertices
uv_{1},uv_{2},uv_{3},uv_{4}
in Figure
20
).

Condition
4
.
As explained earlier, for every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
such that
d_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(

f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, we would like to store the direction of the edge corresponding to
uv_{i}
. For this purpose, we would like to store a point
a\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
,
a\neq d_{f}(uv_{i})
, such that
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
is inside
f
. The line
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
represents the direction of the edge attached to
uv_{i}
from the point
d_{f}(uv_{i})
(e.g., see the pink lines
\ell(c,d_{f}(uv_{2})
,
\ell(c^{\prime},d_{f}(gh_{1})
and
\ell(c^{\prime\prime},d_{f}(mn_{1})
in Figure
20
). So, later, when we glue two “sides” of the edge corresponding to
uv_{i}
, we will verify that the directions of both sided of
uv_{i}
are on the same line, and therefore there is no bend at the point
d_{f}(uv_{i})
.

Definition 45

(
Template for an Info-Frame).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A tuple
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
is an
Info-Frame Template
if the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

f
is a frame.

2.

d_{f}
is a
G^{*}
-drawing on
f
such that for every vertex in
V^{*}
,
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})
, it follows that
(d_{f}(uv_{i}),\{u,v\})
is a turning point in
f
.

3.

U_{f}\subseteq V
and
E_{f}\subseteq\{\{u,v\}\in E^{*}\cup E~|~u\in V(d_{f}),v\in V(d_{f})\cup U_{f}\}
.

4.

For every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
such that

d_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
,

\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
and the following conditions hold:

(a)

\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i})\neq d_{f}(uv_{i})
.

(b)

\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
is inside
f
.

We continue with the next property of an info-frame (Definition
46
). Here, we give the intuition behind the conditions.

Conditions
1
and
1
.
In these conditions, we state additional requirements expected to be satisfied when
F
is meant to describe a part of a drawing of the graph
G
that is bounded by
f
. Recall that
U_{f}
represents the set of vertices that are drawn strictly inside
f
, and
E_{f}
represents the set of edges that are drawn strictly inside
f
, except at least one endpoint on
f
. Thus, we demand that
V(d_{f})\cap U_{f}=\emptyset
and
E_{f}\cap E(d_{f})=\emptyset
(e.g., see Figure
20
).

Condition
2
.
Now, assume that there is an edge
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u
is drawn strictly inside
f
, and
v
is drawn on
f
. There are exactly two cases we consider in Condition
2
. The first case, stated in Condition
2a
, is that the drawing of
\{u,v\}
intersects
f
only at
d(v)
(e.g., see the edge
\{a,b\}
in Figure
20(b)
). In this case, there are no turning points of
\{u,v\}
in
f
other than
(d(u),\{u,v\})
, so
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
. In addition,
\{u,v\}
is drawn strictly inside
f
except at
d(u)
, therefore
\{u,v\}\in E_{f}
. The other case, stated in Condition
2b
, is where the drawing of
\{u,v\}
intersects
f
in points other than
d(u)
. Then, there must be at least one turning point other than
(d(u),\{u,v\})
, so
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})\neq\emptyset
. In addition, in this case, the edge
\{u,v\}
is “divided” into the edges
\{u,uv_{1}\},\{uv_{1},uv_{2}\},\ldots\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}
, so
\{u,v\}\notin E_{f}
. Further,
\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}
is an edge that is strictly drawn inside
f
except at the endpoint
d(uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)})
, so
\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
.

Condition
3
.
This condition is the symmetric version of Condition
2
.

Condition
4
.
Now, assume that there is an edge
\{u,v\}
such that
u
is drawn strictly inside
f
, and
v
is drawn strictly outside
f
, that is,
u\in U_{f}
and
v\notin U_{f}\cup V(d_{f})
. In this case, there must be a turning point of
\{u,v\}
in
f
, therefore
uv_{1}\in V(d_{f})
. In addition,
uv_{1}
is connected with an edge to
u
(that is strictly inside
f
), so
\{u,uv_{1}\}\in E_{f}
(e.g., see the edge
\{u,v\}
in Figure
20
).

Condition
5
.
This condition is the symmetric version of Condition
4
.

Condition
6
.
Now, assume that there is an edge
\{u,v\}
such that
u
and
v
are drawn strictly inside
f
, that is,
u,v\in U_{f}
. If
\{u,v\}
intersects
f
, then there are
\mathsf{index}(u,v)
turning points of
\{u,v\}
in
f
, so
uv_{1},\ldots,uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}\in V(d_{f})
(in particular
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})\neq\emptyset
). In addition,
uv_{1}
and
uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)}
are connected with an edge that is strictly inside
f
to
u
and
v
, respectively. So
\{u,uv_{1}\},\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
, and Condition
6a
is satisfied. Otherwise,
\{u,v\}
does not intersect
f
, so there are no intersection points of
\{u,v\}
in
f
. Thus
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
, and Condition
6b
is satisfied (e.g., see the edge
\{x,w\}
in Figure
20(b)
).

Condition
7
.
Observe that for every
\{u,v\}\in E
,
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{index}(u,v)
, the edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
might be either drawn strictly inside
f
, except for necessarily at the endpoints, or drawn on
f
, but these two cases cannot hold simultaneously. Therefore, if
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d_{f})
, then
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\notin E_{f}
, so Condition
7
is satisfied (e.g., see the edge
\{uv_{3},uv_{4}\}
in Figure
20
).

Definition 46

(
Edges and Vertices of an Info-Frame Property).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A tuple
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
exhibits the
Edges and Vertices of an Info-Frame Property
if
F
is an
Info-Frame Template
and the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

U_{f}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
and
E_{f}\cap E(d_{f})=\emptyset
.

2.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u\in U_{f}
and
v\in V(d_{f})
, exactly one of the following conditions holds.

(a)

U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
and
\{u,v\}\in E_{f}
.

(b)

U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})\neq\emptyset
,
\{u,v\}\notin E_{f}
and
\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
.

3.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
v\in U_{f}
and
u\in V(d_{f})
, exactly one of the following condition holds.

(a)

uv_{1}\notin V(d_{f})
and
\{u,v\}\in E_{f}
.

(b)

\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
,
\{u,v\}\notin E_{f}
and
\{u,uv_{1}\}\in E_{f}
.

4.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u\in U_{f}
and
v\notin U_{f}\cup V(d_{f})
,
\{u,uv_{1}\}\in E_{f}
.

5.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
v\in U_{f}
and
u\notin U_{f}\cup V(d_{f})
,
\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
.

6.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u,v\in U_{f}
, exactly one of the following conditions holds.

(a)

U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})\neq\emptyset
and
\{uv_{\mathsf{index}(u,v)},v\}\in E_{f}
.

(b)

U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
.

7.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
and
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{index}(u,v)
such that
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E(d_{f})
,
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}

\notin E_{f}
.

The next property concerns the vertices
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
such that

d_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. We show that for such a vertex there must be exactly one neighbor in
E_{f}
, and no edges on
f
. Recall that
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
represents a turning point of the edge
\{u,v\}
in
f
and
\{u,v\}
is drawn as a path in
\cal{P}
. So, if
uv_{i}
has an edge on
f
, then one among
d(\{u,v\})
and
f
bends at the point
d_{f}(uv_{i})
, a contradiction (Condition
1
). Now, since
(d_{f}(uv_{i}),\{u,v\})
is a turning point in
f
, then there must be part of
\{u,v\}
that intersects
f
exactly at
d_{f}(uv_{i})
. So,
uv_{i}
has exactly one neighbor in
E_{f}
(Condition
2
).

Definition 47

(
Vertices on Grid Intersection Property).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A tuple
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
exhibits the
Vertices on Grid Intersection Property
if
F
is an
Info-Frame Template
and for every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
such that
d_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\},\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\notin E(d_{f})
.

2.

Exactly one among of the following conditions is satisfied:

(a)

\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i}\}\in E_{f}
.

(b)

\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E_{f}
.

Now, we are ready to define the term info-frame:

Definition 48

(
Info-Frame).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A tuple
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},

\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
is an info-frame if
F
is an
Info-Frame Template
and
F
exhibits the
Edges and Vertices of an Info-Frame Property
and the
Vertices on Grid Intersection Property
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 20
:
(a) An example of an info-frame
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
of a graph
G
with
\{\{a,b\},\{u,v\},\{s,t\},\{y,z\},\{x,w\}\,\{g,h\},\{i,j\}\}\subseteq E
, where the frame
f
is drawn in orange,
d_{f}
is drawn in black,
E_{f}=\{\{u,uv_{1}\},\{uv_{1},uv_{2}\},\{g,gh_{1}\},\{mn_{1},n\},\{st_{3},t\}\}
and
U_{f}=\{a,g,n,t,u,w,x\}
. A cutter
c
of
f
is drawn in green. The vertices which are mapped to points in
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f)\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f)
are shown by hollow squares. The pairs in
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}
and the lines connecting these pairs (representing directions) are shown in pink. (b) A drawing of the info-frame
F
. The drawings of the edges
E_{f}
and the vertices in
U_{f}
are shown in purple.

Now, for later use, define a few additional notations. Let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be two
G^{*}
-drawings and let
P\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
be a set of points. We denote by
V(d,P)
the set of vertices in
V(d)
that are drawn on points in
P
, that is
V(d,P)=\{v\in V(d)~|~
there exists
p\in P
such that
d(v)=p\}
. Next, we define a notation for a case where
d
and
d^{\prime}
“agree” on the points of
P
. That is, for every vertex
u\in V(d,P)
,
d(u)=d^{\prime}(u)
and vice versa. In addition, for every point
p\in P
and an edge
\{u,v\}
,
p
is on the drawing of
\{u,v\}
in
d
if and only if
p
is on the drawn of
\{u,v\}
in
d^{\prime}
. In this case, we say that
d
and
d^{\prime}
are
equal in
P
.

Definition 49

(
Drawings Equality in a Set of Points).

For two
G^{*}
-drawings,
d
and
d^{\prime}
, and a set of points
P\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, we say that
d
and
d^{\prime}
are equal in
P
, denoted by
d(P)=d^{\prime}(P)
, if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

For every
u\in V(d,P)
,
d(u)=d^{\prime}(u)
.

2.

For every
u\in V(d^{\prime},P)
,
d(u)=d^{\prime}(u)
.

3.

For every
p\in P
and
\{u,v\}\in E
,
p\in P(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))
if and only if
p\in P(d^{\prime}(\{uv_{i},

uv_{i+1}\}))
.

Now, we define the concept of a
drawing of an info-frame
. Briefly, a drawing
d
of an info-frame
F
is a drawing exemplifying the “information” encoded by
F
. Since we would like that the drawing would be part of a drawing of the graph
G
, we would like that this drawing would “make sense” as in the following example. Let
\{u,v\}\in E
be an edge, and assume that
u,v\in U_{f}
, that is, the vertices
u
and
v
are to be drawn strictly inside
f
. In addition, assume that
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
, that is, the edge
\{u,v\}
and the frame
f
do not intersect. In this case, we expect that
\{u,v\}
is drawn strictly inside
f
. Therefore,
\{u,v\}\in E(d)
, as stated in Condition
5
(e.g., see the edge
\{x,w\}
in Figure
20(b)
). In addition, recall that in Condition
4
of the definition of
Template for an Info-Frame
(Definition
45
), for every
uv_{i}
drawn on a point from
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{StrictGridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
, we store a direction in
F
. In Definition
47
, we verify that every such vertex corresponding to exactly one edge from the set
E_{f}
, and has no edges on
f
. Thus, the direction is for this unique edge
e
. In particular, we ask that
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
is on the first edge of
d(e)
(e.g., see the direction line
\ell(c,d_{f}(uv_{2}))
and the drawing of the edge
\{uv_{2},uv_{1}\}
in Figure
20(b)
), or the this first edge is on
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
(e.g., see the direction line
\ell(c^{\prime},d_{f}(gh_{1}))
(resp.,
\ell(c^{\prime\prime},d_{f}(mn_{1}))
), and the drawing of the edge
\{uv_{2},uv_{1}\}
(resp.,
\{mn_{1},j\}
) in Figure
20(b)
). This way, we verify that
d(e)
indeed goes to the same direction as
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i})
, as we meant (see Condition
6
). We state these conditions in the next definition.

Definition 50

(
Drawing of an Info-Frame).

Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame and let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing. We say that
d
is a drawing of
F
if the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

d
is bounded by
f
and all the vertices in
V^{*}
of
d
are drawn on
f
.

2.

d_{f}(\mathsf{PlanePoints}(f))=d(\mathsf{PlanePoints}(f))
.

3.

U_{f}
is the set of vertices that are drawn strictly inside
f
in
d
.

4.

E_{f}\subseteq E(d)
is the set of each edge
e
that is drawn strictly inside
f
, except maybe at the endpoints, and at least one endpoint of
e
is drawn on
f
.

5.

For every
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
u,v\in U_{f}
, if
U^{*}_{\{u,v\}}\cap V(d_{f})=\emptyset
, then
\{u,v\}\in E(d)
.

6.

For every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f})\cap V^{*}
such that
d_{f}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(

f_{\mathsf{init}})
,
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),d_{f}(uv_{i}))
is on
\ell(d_{f}(uv_{i}),p_{1})
, or
\ell(d_{f}(uv_{i}),p_{1})
is on
\ell(\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(uv_{i}),

d_{f}(uv_{i}))
, where
z\in\{uv_{i-1},uv_{i+1}\}
such that
\{uv_{i},z\}\in E_{f}
and
d(\{uv_{i},z\})=(d(uv_{i}),p_{1},

\ldots d(z))
.

6.3
Info-Cutter of an Info-Frame

Recall that our algorithm will build parts of drawings from smaller parts of these drawings. Such parts of drawings are described by the info-frames defined in Section
6.2
. Given an info-frame
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
, we would like to know if there exists a drawing of
F
, having already solved this question for every smaller info-frame, that is, every info-frame
F^{\prime}=(f^{\prime},d_{f^{\prime}},E_{f^{\prime}},U_{f^{\prime}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f^{\prime}})
where
f^{\prime}
has fewer grid points in its interior than
f
. Assume that there exists a drawing
d
of
F
. Observe that, if we take a cutter
c
of
f
, we derive two smaller drawings of
d
inside
f
: one inside
f_{1}(c)
and the other inside
f_{2}(c)
(e.g., see Figure
21(b)
). These two drawings can be described by two info-frames,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, respectively. We say that these info-frames are
induced
by
d
and
c
. We will show that every pair of drawings of these two info-frames can be glued together in order to get a drawing of
F
. In light of this, we would like to “guess” (i.e, iterate over) every (small enough) cutter
c
of
f
possible for every drawing that can be described by
F
as well as the two info-frames induced by
d
and
c
. We denote any such guess by
C=(c,F_{1},F_{2})
. We will say that
C=(c,F_{1},F_{2})
is an
info-cutter
of
F
; this term is defined formally later in this subsection.

Now, we define some notations that will be useful later. First, we define a notation weaker than that of equality in Definition
49
. Towards that, consider a drawing
d
of an info-frame
F
, and the two sub-drawings of
d
inside
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
, denoted by
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, respectively. Observe that the intersection of
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
is exactly
c
. Therefore, we expect that
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
“agree” on the part of the drawing that intersects both
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
. Still, observe that there might be a point
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)
and an edge
\{u,v\}\in E
such that
(p,\{u,v\})
is a turning point in
f_{1}
, but not in
f_{2}
. In such a case, we will find a vertex in
V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}
at the point
p
in
d_{1}
, but not in
d_{2}
. That is, for
d_{1}
there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d_{1}(uv_{i})=p
, but for
d_{2}
there is no such
i
. In addition, for
d_{2}
there is a part of the edge
\{u,v\}
whose drawing includes the point
p
in
d_{2}
. That is, there exists an edge
\{uv_{j},uv_{j+1}\}\in E(d_{2})
such that
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d_{2}(\{uv_{j},uv_{j+1}\}))
. Keeping this in mind, we would like to be able to say that the drawings
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are equal on the intersection between them, up to the aforementioned difference. Roughly speaking, we say that
d_{1}(c)=d_{2}(c)

up to renaming
(see Figure
22
). This means that on
c
, the drawings are identical, except maybe with respect to the vertices in
V^{*}
. For the formal definition of this term, recall that we denote by
V(d,P)
the set of vertices in
V(d)
that are drawn on points in
P
, that is
V(d,P)=\{v\in V(d)~|~
there exists
p\in P
such that
d(v)=p\}
.

Definition 51

(
Equality Up To Renaming).

For a set of points
P
, two
G^{*}
-drawings,
d
and
d^{\prime}
, are equal up to renaming in
P
, denoted as
d=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P}d^{\prime}
, if the following conditions hold:

1.

For every
u\in(V(d,P)\cup V(d^{\prime},P))\cap V
,
d(u)=d^{\prime}(u)
.

2.

For every
p\in P
and
\{u,v\}\in E
, there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(uv_{i})=p
or
p\in\mathsf{PlanePoints}(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))
if and only if there exists
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(uv_{j})=p
or
p\in\mathsf{PlanePoints}(d^{\prime}(\{uv_{j},uv_{j+1}\}))
.

Now, assume that we have two
G^{*}
-drawings,
d
and
d^{\prime}
, such that
d=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P}d^{\prime}
for some set of points
P
. Observe that due to Condition
2
of Definition
51
, some of vertices in
V^{*}\cap V(d,P)
might not appear in
d^{\prime}
as vertices from the set
V^{*}
, but as part of an edge, and vice versa (e.g., see vertex
st_{1}
in
d_{f_{2}}
in Figure
21(b)
). We would like to handle this issue by making the drawings more “similar”.
To this end, we present the operation
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d,\{u,v\},p)
. Given a
G^{*}
-drawing
d
, an edge
\{u,v\}\in E
, and a point
p
such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))
, the operation “turns” this point
p
into a vertex from the set
V^{*}
(e.g., see vertex
st_{3}
drawn in green in Figure
22(a)
). Recall that
\ell(c_{j},c_{j+1})
is the line segment joining the points
c_{j}
and
c_{j+1}
. Formally, we define
\mathsf{MakeVer}
as follows.

(a)

(b)

Figure 21
:
Consider the cutter
c
of
f
, shown in green, and the drawing of
F
described in Figure
20(b)
. Here, we illustrate an example of drawings of
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
(shown in (a)), and their corresponding info-frames (shown in (b)). The vertices mapped to points in
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{1})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{1})
and
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{2})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{2})
are marked by hollow squares.

Definition 52

(
\mathsf{MakeVer}
).

Let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing, let
\{u,v\}\in E
, and

let
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

d)
such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which

p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))
. Then,
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d,

\{u,v\},p)
performs the following steps on
d
:

•

For every
i<\ell\leq\mathsf{index}(u,v)
, rename
uv_{\ell}
to
uv_{\ell+1}
.

•

Add the vertex
uv_{i+1}
to
V(d)
, and let
d(uv_{i+1})=p
.

•

Let
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p\in\ell(c_{j},c_{j+1})
where
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})
. Then:

–

Replace the edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+2}\}
by the edges
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
and
\{uv_{i+1},uv_{i+2}\}
in
E(d)
.

–

Update
d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\})=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{j},p)
and
d(\{uv_{i+1},uv_{i+2}\})=(p,c_{j+1}\ldots,c_{r})
in
d
.

It is easy to see that after using this operation, the resulting drawing
d
satisfies the conditions of the definition of a
G^{*}
-drawing (Definition
38
):

Observation 53
.

Let
d
be a
G^{*}
-drawing, let
\{u,v\}\in E
, and let
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d)
such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(d(\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}))
. Then, the drawing obtained by
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d,\{u,v\},p)
is a
G^{*}
-drawing.

Recall that when we examine
d
and
d^{\prime}
such that
d=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P}d^{\prime}
, we would like to make them as similar as possible. To this end, we will use the operation
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d,\{u,v\},p)
for every edge
\{u,v\}
and a point
p\in P
such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which
d^{\prime}(uv_{i})=p
, and there is no
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(uv_{j})=p
, or vice versa. We refer to this overall operation as
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}
:

Definition 54

(
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}
).

Let
P\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
be a set of points and let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be
G^{*}
-drawings such that
d=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P}d^{\prime}
. Then,
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}(d,d^{\prime},P)
performs the following steps:

•

For every edge
\{u,v\}\in E
and a point
p\in P
, such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d^{\prime}(uv_{i})=p
, and there is no
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(uv_{j})=p
, activate
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d,\{u,v\},p)
.

•

For every edge
\{u,v\}\in E
and a point
p\in P
, such that there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d(uv_{i})=p
, and there is no
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d^{\prime}(uv_{j})=p
, activate
\mathsf{MakeVer}(d^{\prime},\{u,v\},p)
.

Let
\hat{d}
and
\hat{d^{\prime}}
the result of the application of
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}(d,d^{\prime},P)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 22
:
An example of renaming. The vertices mapped to points in
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f)\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f)
,
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{1})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{1})
and
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{2})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{2})
are marked by hollow squares. (a) Observe that
d_{f_{1}}=d_{f_{2}}
up to renaming, where
d_{f_{1}}
and
d_{f_{2}}
are the drawings in Figure
21(b)
. Moreover,
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
is the corresponding vertex identification function. Note that, the identification function is only shown for the vertices which are not mapped to
\mathsf{Null}
. Observe that in
d_{f_{1}}
, the vertex
st_{3}
of
d_{f_{1}}
in Figure
21(b)
is renamed
st_{4}
(shown in green here) and a new vertex, shown here in green as well and labeled
st_{3}
, is added, by the
\mathsf{MakeVer}
function. (b) Observe that
d_{f}=d_{f_{2}}
up to renaming, where
d_{f}
and
d_{f_{2}}
are the drawings in Figures
20(a)
and
21(b)
, respectively. Moreover,
\mathsf{Identify}_{d,d_{2}}
is the corresponding vertex identification function. Note that, the identification function is only shown for the vertices which are not mapped to
\mathsf{Null}
.

Now, let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be
G^{*}
-drawings. For later use, we would like to get for every
uv_{i}\in V(d)\cap V^{*}
, its corresponding vertex in
d^{\prime}
, if such vertex exists. That is, a vertex
uv_{j}\in V(d^{\prime})\cap V^{*}
such that
d(uv_{i})=d^{\prime}(uv_{j})
. To this end, we define the function
\mathsf{Identify}_{d,d^{\prime}}:V(d)\cap V^{*}\rightarrow(V(d^{\prime})\cap V^{*})\cup\{\mathsf{Null}\}
as follows. Given
uv_{i}\in V(d)\cap V^{*}
, the function returns
uv_{j}\in V(d)\cap V^{*}
such that
d(uv_{i})=d^{\prime}(uv_{j})
, if such a vertex exists; otherwise, it returns
\mathsf{Null}
. Observe that if such a vertex
uv_{j}
exists, then it is unique. We refer to this function as the
vertex identification function
of
d
and
d^{\prime}
. Formally, we define this function as follows.

Definition 55

(
Vertex Identification Function).

Let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be
G^{*}
-drawings. The vertex identification function of
d
and
d^{\prime}
is the function
\mathsf{Identify}_{d,d^{\prime}}:V(d)\cap V^{*}\rightarrow(V(d^{\prime})\cap V^{*})\cup\{\mathsf{Null}\}
such that for every
uv_{i}\in V(d)\cap V^{*}
,
\mathsf{Identify}_{d,d^{\prime}}(uv_{i})=uv_{j}
for which
d(uv_{i})=d^{\prime}(uv_{j})
, if such
uv_{j}
exists; otherwise,
\mathsf{Identify}_{d,d^{\prime}}(uv_{i})=\mathsf{Null}
.

Observe that the only modifications to the drawings
d
and
d^{\prime}
made by
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}(d,d^{\prime},P)
are done by activating
\mathsf{MakeVer}
. So, due to Observation
53
,
\hat{d}
and
\hat{d^{\prime}}
(defined in Definition
54
) are
G^{*}
-drawings. In addition, observe that for every
uv_{i}\in V(\hat{d},P)\cap V^{*}
there exists
uv_{j}\in V(\hat{d^{\prime}},P)\cap V^{*}
such that
\hat{d}(uv_{i})=\hat{d^{\prime}}(uv_{j})
, and vice versa. Thus, we have the following observation:

Observation 56
.

Let
P\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
be a set of points, let
d
and
d^{\prime}
be two
G^{*}
-drawings such that
d=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P}d^{\prime}
. Then,
\hat{d}
and
\hat{d^{\prime}}
are
G^{*}
-drawings. In addition, for every
uv_{i}\in V(\hat{d},P)\cap V^{*}
,
\mathsf{Identify}_{\hat{d},\hat{d^{\prime}}}(uv_{i})\neq\mathsf{Null}
and for every
uv_{i}\in V(\hat{d^{\prime}},P)\cap V^{*}
,
\mathsf{Identify}_{\hat{d^{\prime}},\hat{d}}(uv_{i})\neq\mathsf{Null}

We now generalize the function
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}
(from Definition
54
) to
k

G^{*}
-drawings,
d_{1},d_{2},

\ldots,d_{k}
such that for every
1\leq i<j\leq k
,
d_{i}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P_{i,j}}d_{j}
where
P_{i,j}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. Our goal is to obtain
k

G^{*}
-drawings
d_{1}^{*},\ldots d_{k}^{*}
, such that the following property holds: for every
1\leq i,j\leq k
and
uv_{\ell}\in V(d_{i}^{*},P_{i,j})\cap V^{*}
, it holds that
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{i}^{*},d_{j}^{*}}(uv_{\ell})\neq\mathsf{Null}
. To this end, we define the function
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}
(defined in Algorithm
1
). For every
1\leq i<j\leq k
iteratively, this function evokes
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}(d_{i}^{*},d_{j}^{*},P_{i,j})
. Initially,
d_{i}^{*}=d_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
, and afterwards
d^{*}_{i}
is updated
k-1
times. By repeatedly updating
d_{i}^{*}
, for each
i\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
, when
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}
terminates, we conclude that the aforementioned property holds.

Definition 57

(
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}
).

Let
d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k}
be
G^{*}
-drawings, and, for every
1\leq i<j\leq k
, let
P_{i,j}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
such that
d_{i}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P_{i,j}}d_{j}
. Then,
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}(d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k},P_{1,2},

P_{1,3},\ldots P_{1,k},

\ldots,P_{i,i+1},\ldots,P_{i,k},\ldots,P_{k-1,k})
is described by Algorithm
1
.

for

1\leq i\leq k

do

1

d^{*}_{i}\leftarrow d_{i}
;

2

end for

3

for

1\leq i<
k

do

4

for

i<j\leq k

do

5

Let
\hat{d^{*}_{i}}
and
\hat{d^{*}_{j}}
the result of the application of
\mathsf{MakeAllVer}(d^{*}_{i},d^{*}_{j},P_{i,j})
;

6

d^{*}_{i}\leftarrow\hat{d^{*}_{i}}
;

7

d^{*}_{j}\leftarrow\hat{d^{*}_{j}}
;

8

end for

9

end for

10

return

(d_{1}^{*},\ldots,d_{k}^{*})
;

11

12

Algorithm 1

\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}(\langle d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k},P_{1,2},P_{1,3},\ldots P_{1,k},\ldots,P_{i,i+1},\ldots,P_{i,k},\ldots,P_{k-1,k}\rangle)

From Observation
56
, after every iteration of Algorithm
1
, for every
i\in[k]
,
d_{i}^{*}
is a
G^{*}
-drawing. So, for every
i\in[k]
,
d_{i}^{*}
returned by
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}
is a
G^{*}
-drawing. In addition, from Observation
56
, for every
1\leq i,j\leq k
and
uv_{\ell}\in V(d_{i}^{*},P_{i,j})\cap V^{*}
,
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{i}^{*},d_{j}^{*}}(uv_{\ell})\neq\mathsf{Null}
, where
d_{i}^{*}
and
d_{j}^{*}
are the drawings returned by
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}
. for each
i\in[k]
. Thus, we have the following observation:

Observation 58
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k}
be
G^{*}
-drawings, and, for every
1\leq i<j\leq k
, let
P_{i,j}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
such that
d_{i}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{P_{i,j}}d_{j}
. Let
d_{i}^{*}
, for each
i\in[k]
, be the result of the application
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}(d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k},P_{1,2},P_{1,3},\ldots P_{1,k},

\ldots,P_{i,i+1},\ldots,P_{i,k},\ldots,P_{k-1,k})
. Then, for each
i\in[k]
,
d_{i}^{*}
is a
G^{*}
-drawing, and for every
1\leq i,j\leq k
and
uv_{\ell}\in V(d_{i}^{*},P_{i,j})\cap V^{*}
, it holds that
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{i}^{*},d_{j}^{*}}(uv_{\ell})\neq\mathsf{Null}
.

6.4
Properties of Info-Cutter

Now, we proceed to define the term
info-cutter
. Towards that, we begin with the definition of its first property. Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame, and let
C=(c,F_{1}=(f_{1}(c),d_{f_{1}},E_{f_{1}},U_{f_{1}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}),F_{2}=(f_{2}(c),d_{f_{2}},E_{f_{2}},U_{f_{2}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}))
. Recall (from the discussion at the beginning of Section
6.3
) that
C
should “reflect” a guess of a cutter
c
of
f
, and the two info-frames induced by a drawing
d
of
F
and
c
. So, we first demand that
c
is indeed a cutter of
f
, and
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are info-frames:

Definition 59

(
Template for an Info-Cutter).

Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame. Then,
C=(c,F_{1}=(f_{1}(c),d_{f_{1}},E_{f_{1}},U_{f_{1}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}),F_{2}=(f_{2}(c),d_{f_{2}},E_{f_{2}},U_{f_{2}},

\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}))
is an
Info-Cutter Template
with respect to
F
if
c
is a cutter of
f
, and
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are info-frames.

Now, recall that since
c
is a cutter of
f
, which splits a drawing of
F
into two drawings, we expect that the “common part” of the drawings of
F_{1}
and
F
will be identical up to renaming. This yields Condition
1
in Definition
60
ahead. Similarly, we expect that the common part of the drawings of
F_{2}
and
F
, and also of the drawings of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, will be identical up to renaming (see the identify functions shown in Figures
22(b)
and
22(a)
, respectively). So, we have Conditions
2
and
3
.

Definition 60

(
Equality of Common Parts).

Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame. Then,
C=(c,F_{1}=(f_{1}(c),d_{f_{1}},E_{f_{1}},U_{f_{1}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}),F_{2}=(f_{2}(c),d_{f_{2}},E_{f_{2}},U_{f_{2}},

\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}))
exhibits
Equality of Common Parts Property
with respect to
F
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

d_{f}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{1})}d_{f_{1}}
, where
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{1})=\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

f_{1}(c))
.

2.

d_{f}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{2})}d_{f_{2}}
, where
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{2})=\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

f_{2}(c))
.

3.

d_{f_{1}}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)}d_{f_{2}}
.

In the following property, we consider the directions of vertices drawn on points from
\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
. First, consider such vertices that are drawn on
c
. Observe that these vertices are in the common part of
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
. As explained in Section
6.2
, we would like the directions of these vertices to be identical. That is, for a vertex
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c))\cap V^{*}
, the direction in
F_{1}
is identical to that of the vertex
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{1}}}(uv_{i})
in
F_{2}
. Now, recall that the direction
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})=a
represents the line
\ell(a,d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i}))
, which is inside
f_{1}(c)
, and hence outside
f_{2}(c)
(e.g., see
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(xw_{1})=c_{1}
in Figure
22(a)
). Similarly,
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f_{2}}}(uv_{i}))=b
represents the line
\ell(b,d_{f_{2}}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f_{2}}}(

uv_{i})))
, which is inside
f_{2}(c)
, and hence outside
f_{1}(c)
(e.g., see
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(xw_{1})=c_{2}
in Figure
22(a)
). We expect that if we attach these two lines together, we get a straight line, so there is no bending at the point
d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})
(e.g.,
c_{1}
,
xw_{1}
and
c_{2}
should be collinear in Figure
22(a)
). Equivalently, we verify that
d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})
is on
\ell(a,b)
. Now, consider a vertex
uv_{i}
drawn on a point from
\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPoints}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
and drawn on
f
. That is, a vertex representing a turning point of
\{u,v\}
in
f
, drawn not on a grid point. Assume that
uv_{i}
is drawn also on
f_{1}(c)
(e.g., see the vertex
mn_{1}
in Figure
22(a)
and
22(b)
). This vertex has directions in both
F
and
F_{1}
, representing the direction of the edge attached to
uv_{i}
from
d_{f}(uv_{i})
to the interior of
f
(e.g., see
c
in Figure
22(b)
), and also to the interior of
f_{1}(c)
(e.g., see
c_{3}
in Figure
22(a)
). Therefore, we expect these two directions to be equal. Nevertheless, since
f_{1}(c)
is smaller and contained inside
f
, the direction of
uv_{i}
in
F
might be too large and it gets out of
f_{1}(c)
(e.g., see the direction line
\ell(c,d_{f}(mn_{1}))
in Figure
22(b)
). Therefore, we ask the direction of
uv_{i}
in
F_{1}
to be on its direction in
F
(e.g., the line
\ell(c_{3},d_{f_{1}}(mn_{1}))
shown in Figure
22(a)
should be contained in the line
\ell(c,d_{f}(mn_{1}))
shown in Figure
22(b)
). Observe that, for our purpose it is enough, since we only aim to make sure that there is no bending of the edge
\{u,v\}
at the point
d_{f}(uv_{i})
.

Definition 61

(
Equality of
V^{*}
Directions).

Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame. Then,
C=(c,F_{1}=(f_{1}(c),d_{f_{1}},E_{f_{1}},U_{f_{1}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}),F_{2}=(f_{2}(c),d_{f_{2}},E_{f_{2}},U_{f_{2}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}))
exhibits
Equality of
V^{*}
Directions
with respect to
F
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

C
exhibits
Equality of Common Parts Property
.

2.

For every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c))\cap V^{*}
such that

d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(

f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}(f_{\mathsf{init}})
,
d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})
is on
\ell(a,b)
, where
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})=a
and
\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}(

\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f_{2}}}

(uv_{i}))=b
.

3.

For every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{1}))\cap V^{*}
, where
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{1})=\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f_{1}(c))
such that

d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}

(f_{\mathsf{init}})
,
\ell(d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(uv_{i}))
is on
\ell(d_{f}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})))
or
\ell(d_{f}

(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f}}

(uv_{i})),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}

(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{1}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})))
is on
\ell(d_{f_{1}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}(uv_{i}))
.

4.

For every
uv_{i}\in V(d_{f_{2}},\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{2}))\cap V^{*}
, where
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{2})=\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f_{1}(c))
such that

d_{f_{2}}(uv_{i})\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPointsIn}(f_{\mathsf{init}})\setminus\mathsf{GridPointSet}

(f_{\mathsf{init}})
,
\ell(d_{f_{2}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}(uv_{i}))
is on
\ell(d_{f}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{2}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{2}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})))
or
\ell(d_{f}

(\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{2}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f}(

\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f_{2}},d_{f}}(uv_{i})))
is on
\ell(d_{f_{2}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}(uv_{i}))
.

Next, recall that
U_{f}
is the set of vertices drawn strictly inside
f
in every drawing of
F
. So, for every drawing
d
of
F
and for every
u\in U_{f}\subseteq V
, we expect that exactly one of the following cases holds:

Case 1.

u
is drawn strictly inside
f_{1}(c)
. Then, as
U_{f_{1}}
is the set of vertices of
V
that are drawn strictly inside the sub-drawing of
d
bounded by
f_{1}(c)
,
u\in U_{f_{1}}
.

Case 2.

u
is drawn strictly inside
f_{2}(c)
. Then, as
U_{f_{2}}
is the set of vertices of
V
that are drawn strictly inside the sub-drawing of
d
bounded by
f_{2}(c)
,
u\in U_{f_{2}}
.

Case 3.

u
is drawn on
c
. Observe that, since the first and last vertices of
c
are drawn on
f
,
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)\neq\emptyset
. So, since
u
is drawn strictly inside
f
and also on
c
,
d(u)=p
for some
p\in\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)
. Therefore, since
d_{f_{1}}
is the drawing on
f_{1}(c)
,
u\in V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))
. Symmetrically,
u\in V(d_{f_{2}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))
. However, since
d_{f_{1}}=_{\mathsf{rename}}^{\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)}d_{f_{2}}
(Condition
3
of Definition
60
),
V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c))\cap V=V(d_{f_{2}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(

c))\cap V
.

So
u\in V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))
implies

u\in V(d_{f_{2}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))
, and vice versa (Condition
1
of Definition
51
).

Thus, we conclude that
U_{f}=(V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)))\cup U_{f_{1}}\cup U_{f_{2}}
(Condition
1
of Definition
62
). Moreover, we expect that these three sets will be distinct. Observe that
V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))\cap U_{f_{1}}=\emptyset
and
V(d_{f_{1}},

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))\cap U_{f_{2}}=\emptyset
trivially, since
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are info-frames (Condition
1
of Definition
45
). So, we only need to demand that
U_{f_{1}}\cap U_{f_{2}}=\emptyset
(Condition
2
of Definition
62
).

Definition 62

(
Partition of
U_{f}
).

Let
F=(f,d_{f},E_{f},U_{f},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f})
be an info-frame. Then,
C=(c,F_{1}=(f_{1}(c),d_{f_{1}},E_{f_{1}},U_{f_{1}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{1}}),F_{2}=(f_{2}(c),d_{f_{2}},E_{f_{2}},U_{f_{2}},\mathsf{V^{*}Dir}_{f_{2}}))
is
U_{f}
- Partitioned
with respect to
F
if:

1.

U_{f}=V(d_{f_{1}},\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)\setminus\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f))\cup U_{f_{1}}\cup U_{f_{2}}
.

2.

U_{f_{1}}\cap U_{f_{2}}=\emptyset
.

We continue with another property of info-cutters, called
Partition of
E_{f}
. Recall that an edge in
E_{f}
is an edge drawn strictly inside
f
except for at least one of its endpoints, which is drawn on
f
, in every drawing of
F
. Here, we consider the way we expect edges from
E_{f}
to “influence” the info-frames
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. For the sake of readability, we split this property into three similar properties. We begin with Part (a) of
Partition of
E_{f}
: the partition of edges in
E_{f}
with both endpoints on
f
. Let
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}\in E_{f}
such that
uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\in V(d_{f})
, that is,
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
is an edge drawn strictly inside
f
except at the endpoints, which are drawn on
f
, in every drawing
d
of
F
(e.g., see the edge
\{uv_{1},uv_{2}\}
in Figure
20(b)
). Recall that for a drawing
d
,
\mathsf{PlanePoints}(d)
is the set of points in the plane that intersect
d
. We denote by
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{1})
,
\mathsf{Common}(f,f_{2})
and
\mathsf{Common}(f_{1},f_{2})
the sets
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f_{1}(c))
,
\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f)\cap\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(f_{2}(c))
and

\mathsf{EnrichedGridPoints}(c)
, respectively. Let
d_{f}^{*},d_{f_{1}}^{*},d_{f_{2}}^{*}
be the result of the application of
\mathsf{MakeAllVerSet}(d_{f},d_{f_{1}},d_{f_{2}},\mathsf{Common}(d_{f},d_{f_{1}}),

\mathsf{Common}(d_{f},d_{f_{2}}),\mathsf{Common}(d_{f_{1}},d_{f_{2}}))
(see Definition
57
). Observe that
d_{f}^{*},d_{f_{1}}^{*},d_{f_{2}}^{*}
are well defined since we assume the satisfaction of the
Equality of Common Parts
property (see Definition
60
). Considering the two frames
f_{1}(c)
and
f_{2}(c)
, we expect that exactly one of the following conditions is satisfied.

Condition
1
.
The edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
is drawn strictly inside
f_{1}(c)
except for both its endpoints, and therefore
\{\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{1}}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{1}}}(uv_{i+1})\}\in E_{f_{1}}
. Observe that in this case, since the edge is drawn strictly inside
f_{1}(c)
except for both its endpoints, then the endpoints are turning points of
\{u,v\}
in
f_{1}(c)
. Thus, there are vertices from the set
V^{*}_{\{u,v\}}
drawn in these points in
d_{f_{1}}
, so
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{1}}}(uv_{i})\neq\mathsf{Null}
and
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{1}}}(uv_{i+1})\neq\mathsf{Null}
.

Condition
2
.
The edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
is drawn strictly inside
f_{2}(c)
except for both its endpoints, and therefore
\{\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{2}}}(uv_{i}),\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{2}}}(uv_{i+1})\}\in E_{f_{2}}
. Observe that, it can be shown that
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{2}}}(uv_{i})\neq\mathsf{Null}
and
\mathsf{Identify}_{d_{f},d_{f_{2}}}(uv_{i+1})\neq\mathsf{Null}
similarly to the previous condition.

Condition
3
.
The edge
\{uv_{i},uv_{i+1}\}
is drawn on
c
, that is,
